       threat attackers modelled on documented intrusion campaigns}

% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\begin{abstract}
% Technical gap + contribution against it, expert reader, as brief as
% possible; ends with the single headline outcome. Written second-last.
%
% PRELIMINARY (Marc, 2026-09-21) --- this is the SEMINAR abstract, verbatim
% (196 words, settled sentence by sentence with Marc; handoff
% 2026-09-21_seminar_title_abstract_design.md §8). It is written for a
% generalist reader and therefore departs from the chapter vocabulary on
% purpose: "simplified attacker" is the baseline attacker, "the new model"
% is the movement attacker, and "Preliminary results" is the seminar's
% hedge, which the dissertation abstract may not keep. The results
% sentences are read from the 100-seed corpus (tab:eff-orderings) and must
% be re-checked at the reported seed count. To be reworked for the expert
% reader and the 300-word budget when the abstract is written in its turn.
In February 2024, US and Australian cyber agencies reported that the
state-sponsored group Volt Typhoon had held access inside American
critical infrastructure networks for at least five years. Advanced
persistent threats like Volt Typhoon run long, staged campaigns with
well-documented tradecraft. Moving target defence (MTD) continually
reconfigures a network so that what an attacker has learned about it
goes out of date before it can be used. Yet simulation studies of MTD
commonly test it against simplified attackers that follow a fixed
sequence of steps. A defence evaluated only against a simplified
attacker gives little confidence against a long, staged campaign. This
work builds an attacker model from published reports of real intrusion
campaigns, with profiles distinguished by operational objective. An
existing MTD simulator is then run under identical conditions, once with
its own simplified attacker and once with the new model. Preliminary
results show that IP shuffling and topology shuffling do the most to
contain the new model's spread through the network. Service diversity
ranks among the best against the simplified attacker but does far less
against the new model. These results make a case for evaluating MTD
against attacker models grounded in documented behaviour.
\end{abstract}

How does moving target defence (MTD) perform against advanced persistent
threat (APT) attackers? This question decomposes into three sub-questions:
\begin{enumerate}[label=SQ\arabic*, ref=SQ\arabic*, leftmargin=*,
                  widest=SQ3, nosep]
  \item\label{sq:capture} How is APT attacker behaviour captured, from
  published campaign intelligence to behavioural attack profiles?
  \item\label{sq:model} How is that behaviour made executable, as an
  attacker that traverses the captured structure inside the simulator?
  \item\label{sq:evaluate} How does MTD performance against the
  resulting attack model differ from its performance against the
  inherited scripted attacker?
\end{enumerate}

\section{Moving target defence}
\label{sec:mtd-concept}

Moving target defence (MTD) is the timely manipulation of system
configurations to modify and control the \emph{attack surface}: the set
of points at which an attacker engages with the system \citep{cho2020}.
It inverts the conventional assumption that vulnerabilities can be
eliminated and system configurations held stable: no system is
perfectly secure, so attacks can be thwarted but not prevented
\citep{ghosh2009nitrd}. By continuously changing the configurations an
attacker relies on, MTD shifts uncertainty onto the attacker, whose
reconnaissance decays faster than it can be acted upon \citep{cho2020}.

The MTD design space is organised around three questions: \emph{what}
to move, \emph{how} to move it, and \emph{when} to move \citep{cho2020}.
The first concerns the configurable attributes available to the
defender: IP addresses, network topologies, operating systems, software
stacks.

The second is captured by the canonical
shuffle--diversity--redundancy (SDR) taxonomy \citep{hongkim2016},
whose three primitives are complementary rather than partitioned
\citep{cho2020}.
\emph{Shuffling} rearranges or randomises existing components (IP
mutation, topology reconfiguration), invalidating reconnaissance the
attacker has already performed. \emph{Diversity} deploys different
implementations of the same function, so that an exploit against one
variant is unlikely to apply to others. \emph{Redundancy} replicates
components to preserve service while the other two operate.

The third question, when to move, distinguishes proactive
(time-triggered), reactive (event-triggered), and hybrid scheduling
regimes \citep{cho2020}. When to move is the tension between cost and
security:
moving too often imposes overhead on legitimate users; moving too
rarely leaves the attacker's reconnaissance valid for longer than it
should be.

MTDSim is a discrete-event simulator built to evaluate MTD mechanisms
singly and in combination \citep{brown2023}.
Figure~\ref{fig:mtdsim-model} draws its three modules, and the
subsections describe them in turn: what network is being defended
(Section~\ref{subsec:network-model}), what defence mechanisms are
implemented and how their deployment is orchestrated
(Section~\ref{subsec:defence-mechanisms}), and what attacker the
defence is evaluated against (Section~\ref{subsec:attacker-model}). The
simulator is the product of four studies over one evolving codebase;
Table~\ref{tab:mtdsim-lineage} lists what each work added to the
codebase, and each addition is described where it is used.

\subsection{Network model}
\label{subsec:network-model}

% Figure 2.2 (new 2026-09-09): the network module opened. Carries what the
% old plate could not fit --- the four host states keyed once, and the two

% Placement [p]: at 16.3 cm plus caption it is over half the text height.
\begin{figure}[p]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{fig_2-2-1a_network_model}
  \caption[The network at three magnifications]{The network at three
  magnifications, each panel opening one element of the panel above it.
  The host layer keys the four states a host can be in; red marks the
  hosts the attacker has taken and the accent its route between them.
  The service layer opens one such host: its operating system holds the
  address and the users' credentials, and runs the services, every one of
  which attaches to an internal target node that carries no service of its
  own. The vulnerability layer opens one service and gives the two
  conditions under which its host falls.}
  \label{fig:network-model}
\end{figure}

% docs/handoffs/2026-08-21_ch2_background_context.md Part 3 (g)): every
% fact re-verified against the code and the write-surface record; items
% 1--8, 10, 12, 13, 16 applied. Two sentences carry wording built from
% Marc's dictated ruling rather than a ratified proposal --- RATIFY ON
% READ: the levels-of-subnets sentence (item 10) and the endpoints-persist
% sentence (item 6); P2 opener split into three sentences and the P4
% closer cut on Marc's follow-up ruling (2026-08-27). Depth is now "level" (Brown's word; HARM keeps
% "layer"). Exploit time dropped by ruling (the movement attacker supplies
% its own times). FLAG: the compromise rule is stated as the code does it
% --- per-service impact sum past a threshold, host falls on a compromised
% service adjacent to the internal target node --- which differs from
% Marc's dictated recollection (no accumulation, a dependency chain); see
% the ledger. Citations barabasi1999 / watts1998 taken from Brown's
% reference list; hongkim2012harm pages set from Cho 2020 --- all three
% VERIFY (Marc). Pending: forward-use test against fig:mtdsim-model once
% the figure's two booked errors (layer names; attack-tree vulnerability
% layer) are fixed; integration check (P2 claim-first, endpoint sentence
% placement).

The network in MTDSim is a three-layer hierarchical attack representation
model (HARM) \citep{hongkim2012harm}: a graph of graphs. The host layer is
the topology, where the hosts are nodes and reachability between them is
the edges. The service layer is what is running on each host. The
vulnerability layer is the vulnerabilities each service carries, and the
attacker compromises from the bottom upwards.

On the host layer, hosts are placed in levels of depth. Each level is a set
of subnets constructed using the Barab\'asi--Albert model
\citep{barabasi1999} and joined to the level before it. The construction
mimics the scale-free characteristic of a real-world network
\citep{brown2023}. The first level is the exposed
endpoints, and reaching a deeper level requires compromising a host in the
level before it. By default the network has 50 hosts in four levels of
depth and eight subnets, with five exposed endpoints and five database hosts
at the deepest level.
% [2026-09-20: P4 of the §5.2 split-stream scrutiny (setup handoff §AL1 1d, §AM),
%  session-drafted, accepted by Marc in chat: §5.2's "the simulator's own
%  default" pointed here and nothing here stated the values. Verified at
%  mtdnetwork/component/time_network.py:11-12. Ratify on read.]
On the service layer each host has one operating system
and a small number of versioned services, the services connected in a
service graph constructed using the Watts--Strogatz model
\citep{watts1998}. Each host also carries users and credentials, and an IP
address. The endpoints are never rewritten: they persist unchanged through
the life of the simulation while everything around them can move, because
they are the ingress into the network, so the MTD mechanisms protect the
interior, not the perimeter.

Each vulnerability is priced by a CVSS complexity, which sets how likely an
exploit is to succeed. A service is compromised when the impact of its
exploited vulnerabilities accumulates past its threshold, and the host
falls when a compromised service is adjacent to the host's internal target
node. The vulnerabilities are synthetic: they are generated, not drawn from
any real-world vulnerability database.

The attacker never sees the whole topology. It only sees a visible
subgraph, which comprises the endpoints plus all the compromised hosts and
all of their neighbours.

\label{subsec:defence-mechanisms}

% Table cells are fact-scaffold from the write-surface census and the
% 2026-08-27 pool rulings --- ratify on read. Ruled 2026-09-02: caption
% cite; hierarchy class -> layer -> mechanism; lineage column removed
% (the lineage-pool fact returns to prose/ch4).
\begin{table}[htbp]
  \centering
  \caption[The seven defence mechanisms]{What to
  move and how: the seven defence mechanisms, from the pool of
  Brown~\citep{brown2023}, added to by Zhang~\citep{zhang2023},
  grouped by class and by what each rewrites.}
  \label{tab:defence-mechanisms}
  % Restyled to the house table style 2026-09-05 (Marc's design, after
  % Table 3.1): the SDR class carried as a rotated group label, no
  % header over it; one zebra row per mechanism. Ruled 2026-09-07
  % (Marc): the layer is repeated on every row rather than keyed by a
  % sub-block's first row --- per-row stripes and a per-block key
  % contradicted each other, and a repeated key makes the layer a row
  % attribute, not a third level (conventions §k rule 4).
  \tablestyle
  \begin{tabular}{@{}cP{2.4cm}P{3.1cm}P{8.6cm}@{}}
    \toprule
    & \textit{What} to move & Mechanism & Description \\
    \midrule
    & host layer & IP shuffle & reassigns every internal host's address \\
    & host layer & Complete topology shuffle & regenerates the graph; host instances and their compromise state kept \\
    & host layer & Host topology shuffle & swaps hosts' positions within a level of depth \\
    & service layer & Port shuffle & moves every non-target service's port on internal hosts; services and vulnerabilities untouched \\
    \rowgroup{5}{Shuffle} & credentials & User shuffle & redraws each internal host's user accounts from the network's pool \\
    \midrule
    & service layer & OS diversity & changes each internal host's operating system and redraws the services the new OS cannot run; exploits are OS-specific, so known vulnerabilities may stop applying \\
    \rowgroup{2}{Diversity} & service layer & Service diversity & redraws every non-target service on internal hosts \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

% Strategies table --- cells are fact-scaffold, ratify on read. Ruled
% 2026-09-02: blocks 3+4 conjoined as one float; regimes proactive /
% hybrid (selector reclassified vs Tay's "reactive" --- Cho's reactive
% is detection-triggered, absent here; booked for a ch3 clause and a
% record consistency sweep); name MTDShield (Tay's own); covering term
% "deployment strategies"; absence sentences to prose.
\begin{table}[htbp]
  \centering
  \caption[The five deployment strategies]{The five deployment strategies, grouped by scheduling regime; the
  four execution schemes, as termed by Zhang~\citep{zhang2023}, and MTDShield, the
  reinforcement-learning selector of Tay~\citep{tay2024}.}
  \label{tab:deployment-strategies}
  \tablestyle
  \begin{tabular}{@{}llP{0.62\textwidth}@{}}
    \toprule
    \textit{When} to move & Execution scheme & Description \\
    \midrule
    proactive & random & draws one mechanism from the pool at random on each interval \\
    & alternative & rotates through the pool in a fixed order, one per interval \\
    & simultaneous & deploys every mechanism on each interval \\
    & single & deploys one fixed mechanism throughout the run \\
    \midrule
    hybrid & MTDShield & re-evaluates on a fixed tick: a double deep Q-network reads the network's security metrics and selects one mechanism to deploy, or no operation, so movement follows the observed posture rather than the schedule \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

% Dictated 2026-09-02; passes 2+3a, 3b, 4 (split-stream), 5
% (split-stream), and 6 (split-stream voice pass) all applied
% 2026-09-02; pass-6 ledger V1-V12 + registry rows blanket-accepted.
% DO NOT RE-FLAG: the endpoint "although the topology around them can
% move" rider (W-6 repair --- two black boxes have proposed cutting it;
% it scopes the claim); the lineage-pool / four-of-seven clause
% (explained through citations, ch4's job); C2-A/C3-A pass-5 cuts.
% Person regime: simulator facts artefact-subject (V11). Gate: passes
% post-ledger. Next: integration check (both openers re-perform 2.1's
% frame --- ordering call, not this unit's).
% The question is how MTD mechanisms are implemented (the what and the
% how) and orchestrated (the when) in MTDSim.
MTDSim implements shuffle
and diversity only; there is no redundancy. These two families are
split on attacker effect (Table~\ref{tab:defence-mechanisms}): shuffle
renames or relocates resources without changing what they can do, and
diversity changes the software stack so that found exploits no longer
apply. The optimised OS assignment variant \citep{zhang2023} has been
withdrawn from the mechanism pool: it added overhead without providing
the diversity-assignment value. Across all seven mechanisms, the endpoints are not
manipulated, so their attack surface remains the same throughout the
attack campaign, although the topology around them can move
(Section~\ref{subsec:network-model}).

% That is the what and the how; when do we move? 
There are five
deployment strategies in MTDSim (Table~\ref{tab:deployment-strategies}).
Four are time-triggered, or proactive; one is hybrid: MTDShield, Tay's
reinforcement-learning selector \citep{tay2024}. \emph{No operation} is a
policy choice: MTDShield chooses one of four of the MTD mechanisms, or
nothing. That is the when not to move. The interval between deployments
is 200\,s plus a small exponentially drawn term, half a second on average,
so deployments arrive close to periodically. A deployment takes time to
complete, from 20\,s to 110\,s depending on the mechanism; four of the
seven values are Zhang's \citep{zhang2023} and the rest are the simulator's.
% [2026-09-20: P3 of the §5.2 split-stream scrutiny (setup handoff §AL1 1c, §AM),
%  session-drafted, accepted by Marc in chat. Replaces "is drawn exponentially
%  [zhang2023]", which Table 5.2 contradicted (exponential is its VARIANT level);
%  closes the ch2 correction owed since the 2026-09-09 scrutiny pass (C).
%  time_generator.py:9-11 (loc + Exp(0.5)); constants.py MTD_TRIGGER_INTERVAL,
%  MTD_DURATION; provenance.md IS-TIM-03 (Zhang's table covers four). Ratify on read.]
Two mechanisms rewriting the
same layer cannot operate concurrently \citep{zhang2023}. There are no
purely reactive deployment strategies in the simulator: no detection
channel is encoded, so a detection-triggered strategy, such as an
IDS-based scheme, is not possible.

\subsection{Attacker model}
\label{subsec:attacker-model}

\begin{figure}[p]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{fig_2-2-3a_attacker_model}
  \caption[What the baseline attacker holds, and how it proceeds]{What the
  baseline attacker holds, and how it proceeds. The upper panel gives what
  a defence rewrite leaves it: the objective, the hosts and the credentials

  neighbours, and a dashed edge is where a rewrite sends it back to.}
  \label{fig:attacker-model}
\end{figure}


% DRAFT STATE (2026-08-27): dictated by Marc, repaired (pass 2+3a) and
% scrutinised (pass 4) the same session; 3b markers and VERIFY items
% below are open. Two floats (states table, objectives table) carry the
% procedure and the scenarios so the prose carries rules and assumptions.

% RULED 2026-09-05: "threat model" -> "attacker model" (thesis-wide
% standardisation, terminology.md row ratified). Noun swap only.
The attacker model that existed originally in the simulator is what this
thesis terms the baseline attacker. It is scripted: every attacker is identical
and uniform in skill \citep{brown2023}. It is
implemented as a closed six-state machine:
Table~\ref{tab:attacker-states} lists the states and
Figure~\ref{fig:attacker-model} draws it; the states represent the attack
phases. This finite state machine is inspired by Lockheed Martin's Cyber
Kill Chain and MITRE ATT\&CK \citep{brown2023, hutchins2011,
strom2018mitre}. The attacker holds an objective, of which two exist
(Table~\ref{tab:attacker-objectives}); Brown \citep{brown2023} gives the
implementation detail.
% [pass 5 compress PROPOSED, unruled] cut "Brown ... gives the
% implementation detail" and carry the citation on the objective clause
% instead: "of which two exist (Table 2.3) \citep{brown2023}." --- the
% pointer clause fails survival test 3; the citation must survive the cut.
% [pass 6 ruled 2026-08-27] bridge inserted in Marc's words (states
% represent the attack phases); objective sentence moved up (reorder).
% [M2, ruled 2026-08-27] the targeted objective is described as live by
% Marc's ruling --- his work relies on it; the record (IS-SCN-03,
% targeted_objective_probe §1) still says vestigial and is to be fixed by
% him, not by a session.

\begin{table}[htbp]
  \centering
  \caption[The six states of the baseline attacker]{The six states of
  the baseline attacker, named as in the simulator's code, and the rule
  each applies. Transitions are drawn in Figure~\ref{fig:attacker-model}.}
  \label{tab:attacker-states}
  \tablestyle
  \begin{tabular}{@{}lP{0.76\textwidth}@{}}
    \toprule
    State & Description \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{SCAN\_HOST} & builds the target queue: the endpoints plus the uncompromised neighbours of compromised hosts, nearest to the foothold first; nothing found ends the run \\
    \texttt{ENUM\_HOST} & takes the next host from the queue; ten enumerations of one host and it is given up \\
    \texttt{SCAN\_PORT} & discovers the reachable services; harvested credentials are tried first, and a match compromises the host without an exploit \\
    \texttt{EXPLOIT\_VULN} & attempts each service's top vulnerabilities by return on attack; the host falls on accumulated impact (Section~\ref{subsec:network-model}) \\
    \texttt{BRUTE\_FORCE} & one probabilistic login attempt, its likelihood rising with the users already held; failure abandons the host \\
    \texttt{SCAN\_NEIGHBOR} & after a compromise, the new host's neighbours join the front of the queue \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[htbp]
  \centering
  \caption[The two objectives of the baseline attacker]{The two
  objectives of the baseline attacker, Brown's general and target
  attack scenarios \citep{brown2023}; the opportunistic objective's
  termination ratio is Zhang's \citep{zhang2023}.}
  \label{tab:attacker-objectives}
  \tablestyle
  \begin{tabular}{@{}lP{0.80\textwidth}@{}}
    \toprule
    Objective & Description \\
    \midrule
    Opportunistic & compromise the network: 80\,\% of the hosts, or until nothing new is discovered \\
    Targeted & compromise a target host, deep-embedded, such as a database \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

The attacker advances using reachability and CVSS. It exploits
vulnerabilities in services, increasing its impact, eventually
compromising the host, and reaching the host's neighbours. When a
compromise is successful, the attacker scans the neighbours of the host,
returns to enumerating the next host, and starts scanning its ports. The
knowledge the attacker holds is the visible subgraph
(Section~\ref{subsec:network-model}), and this grows outwards from its
foothold.
% [pass 6 ruled 2026-08-27] reorder: pivot and visible-subgraph sentences
% joined to the walk; "and expanding its reachable set" cut (duplicate of
% "grows outwards from its foothold"). Triad ruled keep (pass 4).
% [EXPAND, PROPOSED --- unruled] with the possessions sentence cut, "keys"
% has no referent in prose (credentials sit only in Table 2.2). One clause
% from Marc's earlier dictation would close it: "... and that is the keys:
% every user on the host, reusable across the network." Wording his.

A network-layer mechanism disrupts the attacker at any state; an
application-layer one only while it is scanning ports, exploiting, or
brute-forcing. After a network-layer mechanism the attacker returns to
scanning hosts, after an application-layer one to scanning ports
\citep{brown2023}. Compromised hosts stay compromised \citep{zhang2023}.

% since pass 5 (C3-A, 2026-09-02); "layer" sense collision deferred to pass 6.
% [WRONG, minor] "at any one of these states" over-claims: a network-layer
% mutation interrupts any state, an application-layer one only scan
% ports / exploit / brute force (mtd_operation.py:223-236; Zhang Fig 7).

This chapter surveys the literature on evaluating MTD against APT
attackers, along the three sub-questions of
Chapter~\ref{ch:intro}. Section~\ref{sec:apt-survey} surveys how APT
attacker behaviour is captured (\ref{sq:capture});
Section~\ref{sec:mtd-evaluation-survey} how the field evaluates a
defence mechanism (\ref{sq:evaluate}); Section~\ref{sec:mtd-attacker-models}
how the attacker is modelled in those evaluations (\ref{sq:model}),
last because it closes on the gap that motivates
Chapter~\ref{ch:attacker-model}.

\section{Survey of APT attackers}
\label{sec:apt-survey}

This section surveys the literature for whether a machine-usable
specification of an APT campaign can be produced from it, and which
elements such a specification still lacks. Section~\ref{subsec:apt}
defines the APT attacker and its lifecycle; Section~\ref{subsec:attack}
the knowledge base its behaviour is recorded in;
Section~\ref{subsec:profiling} the methods that reconstruct that
behaviour as structured representations, and their current limitations.

% 1 unit. Review Sec. III-C, tightened. [Flag resolved 2026-09-02, Marc's

% the duplicated halves of S2/S3 cut. Subsection shape is now
% definition -> lifecycle -> G5 current reporting.]
\subsection{Advanced persistent threat}
\label{subsec:apt}

An APT is a class of cyber threat in which a well-resourced attacker,
typically nation-state or organisation-sponsored, pursues a specific
objective against a specific target over an extended time horizon,
retooling at a tempo that outpaces
reactive defence \citep{alshamrani2019}. The three terms name three
properties: \emph{advanced} tooling and tradecraft, a \emph{persistent} low-and-slow tempo
that trades speed for evasion across months or years, and a
\emph{threat} defined by its objective: data exfiltration, impediment
of mission-critical components, or positioning for future operations
\citep{alshamrani2019}. NIST states the same profile behaviourally: an
APT attacker pursues its objectives repeatedly, adapts to the defender's
resistance, and sustains the interaction needed to meet its goal
\citep{nist2011sp80039}.
% [terminology ruled 2026-09-02: "APT actor" -> "APT attacker"
% (registry row ratified; Alshamrani's own phrase). "Volt Typhoon
% actors" below stays --- source-echo carve-out.]
% [G7a resolved 2026-09-02] direct cite of SP 800-39's APT definition
% (App. B, p. B-1): "(i) pursues its objectives repeatedly over an
% extended period of time; (ii) adapts to defenders' efforts to resist
% it; and (iii) is determined to maintain the level of interaction
% needed to execute its objectives". Paraphrase checked against the PDF
% Marc supplied; bib entry activated from its VERIFY stub, Marc to sight.
% [E1 CUT, ruled by Marc 2026-09-02 (pass 5 elastic): "This is
% precisely what the commodity attacker is not: single-run,
% smash-and-grab operations that neither hide nor adapt and end at
% first detection \citep{alshamrani2019}." TECHNICAL-CLAIM FLAG: the
% transience foil (single-run / non-adaptive / ends at detection) now
% has no prose home --- the 3.1 interstitial's commodity contrast is
% heterogeneity, a different axis; carrier is the alshamrani2019
% citation. E2 ruled same day: the espionage-contrast sentence KEEPS
% its pared (C7) form --- do not re-flag.]

APT operations follow a five-phase lifecycle:
reconnaissance, foothold establishment, lateral movement, exfiltration
or impediment, and post-operation cleanup \citep{alshamrani2019}. The
first two phases are invariant; the remaining three are contingent on
the attacker's objective (positioning for future operations may
forgo exfiltration entirely) \citep{alshamrani2019}.

APT attackers are still being documented
today. Mandiant's M-Trends 2026 report places the global median
dwell time across all intrusions at 14 days; espionage-class
intrusions sit at a median of 122 days \citep{mtrends2026}.
% [M1a IMPLEMENTED 2026-09-02, Marc's ruling ("propose a more nuanced,
% more accurate sentence") --- SESSION-PROPOSED WORDING, ratify-on-read.
% Carries the nuance: 14 d = all intrusions (eCrime-dominated
% population), 122 d = the espionage class. Also resolves the [3b]
% stage direction by naming M-Trends. Original dictated sentence: "For
% example, this CTI vendor report reports that APT attacks have a
% global median dwell time of 14 days and espionage-class intrusions
% 122 days". Grounding: breach_reports_macro_timing.md.]
The pacing depends on the objectives. Volt Typhoon established
footholds in victim IT environments for at least five
years \citep{cisaaa24038a}: living off the
land and valid accounts.

NTDS.dit, the Active Directory database, was dumped from three domain
controllers over a four-year
period; Volt Typhoon actors were largely silent after credential
dumping, performing discovery without bulk exfiltration: persistence
maintained and revalidated.

% use by the advisory's own apposition ("the Active Directory

% [3b ruled 2026-09-02, accepted: seam kept; "from three domain

% persistence maintained and revalidated".]
That is pre-positioning as an objective, for possible future
operational technology (OT) disruption.
% [pass 6 P3 applied 2026-09-02, accepted: OT spelled at first use.]
% [3b ruled 2026-09-02, accepted: doubled halves cut to one --- the

% next sentence. Flip if the agencies-attribution should be in prose.]
This is not consistent with traditional cyber espionage, where there is
exfiltration over a shorter time frame \citep{cisaaa24038a}.

% [3b ruled 2026-09-02, accepted: "impact" -> "exfiltration" (the
% advisory's contrast class is espionage/intelligence gathering, i.e.

Pre-positioning is NIST's third objective class \citep{nist2011sp80039}.

\subsection{MITRE ATT\&CK}
\label{subsec:attack}

MITRE ATT\&CK is a knowledge base of attacker behaviours derived from
real-world observations, developed by the MITRE Corporation in 2013
\citep{strom2018mitre}. ATT\&CK has
become the lingua franca for attacker behaviour
\citep{ferraz2026, buchel2025}.
% [moved 2026-09-02 restructure: the lingua-franca sentence forward
% from mid-P2 --- the subsection's purpose sentence, now stated before
% the mechanics rather than after them.]
% [P1 reshape, Marc's 2026-09-02 feedback walk: purpose of P1 = what
% ATT&CK is + why it matters + what is in it. Order: knowledge base ->
% contents (matrix + catalogues, groups as a concept only) -> domain
% decomposition -> Enterprise richest. The earlier [GAP] (name the
% matrix) is filled by the merged contents sentence below.]
The knowledge base holds the attack matrix alongside catalogues of
named attacker groups (\attackid{G1017} for Volt Typhoon), software
(\attackid{S0002} for Mimikatz, in Volt Typhoon's toolkit), and
campaigns (\attackid{C0035} for its KV Botnet Activity), each
group characterised by the techniques observed across its operations
\citep{mitre2026attackkb, alsada2024}.
% [cite placement 2026-09-02 (Marc's ruling, citation review): the
% knowledge base vouches for the catalogue entries, Al-Sada for the
% characterisation claim.]
% [3b --- merge joint, flagged: "The knowledge base holds the attack
% matrix alongside catalogues of" replaces "Beyond the matrix itself,
% ATT&CK catalogues" (your ruling: the matrix must be named before
% anything is "beyond" it); the joint words are session-chosen ---
% yours to keep or re-noun.]
% [3b --- examples rethreaded through Volt Typhoon (your 2026-09-02
% ruling: group / its software / its campaign, the recurring
% illustration): G0016 APT29 -> G1017 Volt Typhoon; S0154 Cobalt
% Strike -> S0002 Mimikatz (in the bundle's G1017 uses-list; picked
% for reader recognisability over the LOTL system tools also listed);
% C0024 SolarWinds Compromise -> C0035 KV Botnet Activity (one of two
% campaigns the bundle attributes to G1017; the other is C0039 Versa
% Director Zero Day Exploitation). All three verified against the
% pinned v19.1 bundle this session. Connectives "in Volt Typhoon's
% toolkit" / "for its" are session-chosen --- yours.]
The attack matrix decomposes into Enterprise, ICS, and Mobile; among
public exports, the Enterprise bundle preserves the richest
campaign-level structure \citep{ferraz2026}.
% [3b --- assembled: first clause repaired from your 2026-09-02 spoken
% feedback ("the attack matrix decomposes into Enterprise ICS and
% Mobile"); second clause is the existing richest clause with "STIX"
% deleted ("among public STIX exports"), its definition having moved
% to 3.1.3 --- "exports"/"bundle" now lean on a serialisation defined
% later; keep or re-noun, yours.]

% [P2 --- the matrix. Restructure ruling 2026-09-02: the hierarchy
% teaching now follows the status paragraph (big to small).]
ATT\&CK
organises behaviour into a four-level hierarchy of tactics, techniques
and sub-techniques, and procedures. A tactic refers to \emph{what} an
attacker does to achieve a goal; a technique refers to \emph{how} that
tactic is realised; a sub-technique refines a technique into a specific
variant; a procedure is a particular instance of a technique, capturing
the steps by which it has been implemented \citep{rodriguez2024}. The
Initial Access tactic (\attackid{TA0001}), for example, may be realised
through Valid Accounts (\attackid{T1078}), via the Domain Accounts
sub-technique (\attackid{T1078.002}), with a procedure recording Volt
Typhoon's use of
compromised domain accounts to authenticate to devices on compromised
networks \citep{mitre2026attackkb} (Figure~\ref{fig:attack-matrix}).
% [repair 2026-09-02, flagged: the figure pointer folded in as a
% parenthetical --- the compress licence's mechanical fold.]
% [3b --- re-walked per your 2026-09-02 ruling: the Phishing/T1566.001
% walk swapped onto the figure's recurring-illustration path (Valid
% Accounts -> Domain Accounts -> Volt Typhoon); the procedure clause
% is recast from MITRE's own G1017 procedure example on the T1078.002
% page ("has used compromised domain accounts to authenticate to
% devices on the compromised networks") --- your frame, MITRE's facts;
% ratify the recast.]
Behaviour at the technique-and-tactic level is stable enough to model
an attacker against because it captures the persistent operational
habits of APT attackers, distilled into the matrix from observed
operations.

% [PASS 4 M1 RULED 2026-09-03 ("track the paper properly"): the trait
% triple restored to Rodr\'iguez's verbatim wording --- the draft's
% "objectives, motivations, and knowledge" was a paraphrastic
% substitution the extraction had already flagged (rodriguez2024.md
% Concept 2 / Concern 1: the paper's \S7 reads "objectives, knowledge,
% and modus operandi"). Do not re-flag.]
% [PASS 6 P1-signpost RULED 2026-09-03 (voice-pass, accepted): "ATT&CK's"
% prepended to the TTP list --- ties the section's opening definition
% back to 3.1.2's subject (the "what is it relating to" gap in Marc's
% read). Deletion/insertion of his own bridge noun.]
Attack profiling captures attacker behaviour as structured
representations: connecting ATT\&CK's tactics, techniques, and
procedures into chains shaped by the attacker's objectives, knowledge,
and modus operandi \citep{rodriguez2024}.
% [DICTATED 2026-09-03 (slot 1: the so-what), repaired per pass 2+3a.
% Repair log: self-correction "execute sorry operationalise" resolved
% to the second form; "known defence mechanisms" (STT of "you know,
% defence mechanisms") -> "defence mechanisms" --- VERIFY; deletions
% "by"/"its"/"this" close the dangler ("by producing its ... this
% allows us"); pads dropped. The first formulation's reuse point is
% carried by "reusable". SCRUTINY: claim is in scope (matches the
% preamble's framing, no overclaim); "reusable" now appears here AND
% in the next sentence --- TIGHTEN candidate for pass 5.]
% [PASS 6 RULED 2026-09-03 (voice-pass, accepted): V1 "these profiles"
% -> "them" (doubled noun, pass-2 self-repair residue); V6 "moving
% target defence" -> "MTD" (defined at intro l.111 --- abbreviation
% after definition).]
Reusable attack profiles can be operationalised for the evaluation
of defence mechanisms such as MTD.
% [C1 cut here (the APT well-documented/recurrent sentence, the
% ported 3.1.1-dissolve S4) --- see the pass-5 log above. X2: the
% paragraphs joined.]
The persistent
obstacle is a procedural-semantic gap: CTI describes \emph{what}
attackers do but routinely omits the sequencing, preconditions, and
dependencies (the \emph{how}) required to operationalise behaviour as
a simulable process \citep{ferraz2026}.
% [M-B ACCEPTED 2026-09-03: subject repaired "These catalogues" ->
% "ATT\&CK's catalogues" (session-applied from the chat candidates).
% [3b] confirm the word on your walk. CITED (pass 4 M3, 2026-09-03):
% both prior candidates checked and neither carries the
% interchange-format claim per their extractions; a DRAFT bib entry
% for the OASIS STIX 2.1 standard itself (oasis2021stix) now carries
% it --- entry fields from session knowledge, marked VERIFY in the
% bib, confirm on read.]
ATT\&CK's catalogues are serialised in Structured Threat Information
Expression (STIX) 2.1 \citep{oasis2021stix}.
% [F2 pass 5: the standard-interchange status clause cut; the claim
% moves to the oasis2021stix citation.]
% [M-A ACCEPTED 2026-09-03: opener "Yet this serialisation, the
% ATT\&CK-in-STIX export, records" -> "The ATT\&CK-in-STIX export
% records" --- deletion only; the sentence now reads as the gap's
% concrete instance. Do not re-flag the general/instance adjacency.
% Q1 pass 5: the which/how frame deleted; the enumeration stands.]
The ATT\&CK-in-STIX export encodes no temporal order, explicit
prerequisites, environmental constraints, or branching logic between
techniques \citep{ferraz2026}.

% [PASS 6 RULED 2026-09-03 (voice-pass, accepted): F1 REVIVED at the
% P2 head, superseding the pass-5 F1 cut. Marc's read named the
% automated->manual pivot as weak; the two-forms fork restores the
% antecedent that makes "that manual path's exemplar" land and gives
% "neither strand" (exit paragraph) its introduction --- fixing the
% pivot, interaction (i), and the missing frame in one move. His own
% words, restored verbatim.]
Bridging this gap takes two forms, manual curation and automated
extraction, that trade coverage against fidelity. Automated extraction
dominates the literature \citep{buchel2025} and spans three families:
natural language processing (NLP), process mining (PM), and large
language models (LLMs).
% [M-E ACCEPTED 2026-09-03, both trims applied --- do not re-flag:
% (a) PM tail "; the manual cost shifts from narrative curation to
% authoring and maintaining the technique-labelling rules" cut.
% TECHNICAL-CLAIM FLAG: the automation-isn't-free counterweight loses
% its carrier; the SoK verdict still carries the paragraph's
% conclusion. (b) NLP tail ", surfacing chains such as phishing
% followed by macro execution that individual reports describe in
% isolation" cut (second example; the 713/124 numbers stay).
% R1 pass 5: the PM sentence M-E(a) trimmed is now cut whole --- see
% the pass-5 log.]
% [PASS 6 RULED 2026-09-03 (voice-pass, accepted): V2 --- the naked
% "it" (plural subject "approaches ... combine", singular verb
% reaching into the appositive for ChronoCTI) given its head noun;
% the duplicated \citep{rahman2025} after "ChronoCTI" removed, the
% end-of-sentence cite covers both claims.]
NLP-based approaches, exemplified by ChronoCTI,
combine domain-fine-tuned language models with supervised classifiers
to extract structured behaviour from text at scale: applied to 713
reports, ChronoCTI mines 124 recurring temporal patterns
\citep{rahman2025}.
LLM-based pipelines,
exemplified by AttacKG+ \citep{zhangY2025}, replace the trained
classifier with prompted LLM stages. However, a
Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) on TTP extraction finds that
generative and embedder-based methods do not yet beat traditional NLP
classifiers in realistic open-set evaluation: even on the 50 most
common TTPs, the best systems plateau near $F_1 = 0.70$
\citep{buchel2025}.
% [M-C ACCEPTED 2026-09-03: tail ", at the coverage cost noted above"
% cut (referent died with cut (3); the claim's one carrier is the
% corpus landing sentence). Do not re-flag.]
% [PASS 4 minor SCOPE, ACCEPTED 2026-09-03: the verdict's subject
% narrowed --- the SoK's protocol covers NER / embedder / generative
% text extraction, not process mining (buechel2025.md), so "it" swept
% PM under a verdict never rendered on it. Session words "its
% text-extraction families" --- ratify on read.]
Automated extraction holds the coverage advantage,
but on the SoK's evidence its text-extraction families do not yet
recover technique-level behaviour reliably enough to ground an
attacker on; manual curation supplies that fidelity.

% [slot 2 --- session-proposed pivot RATIFIED in chat 2026-09-03
% ("just accept"); resolves the manual-path / "this missing
% structure" antecedent [3b]. Session words, ratify-on-read stands.
% Q2 pass 5: pivot merged into the definition sentence (pivot words
% verbatim as the head; "supplies exactly this missing structure,"
% deleted); R8: "in 2022" deleted.]
That manual path's exemplar is Attack Flow, a STIX 2.1 extension
language released by MITRE Engenuity's Center for Threat-Informed
Defense (CTID), representing the sequence and dependency relationships
between techniques observed in real incidents
\citep{ctid2025attackflow}.
% [M-D RULED 2026-09-03, INVERTED from the session's proposal: the
% content lives in the PROSE; the caption is stripped lean (Marc:
% caption is AI-generated context, boil it to one sentence). This
% sentence stays in full. Do not re-flag the prose/caption overlap
% --- the caption side was the one cut.
% F3 pass 5: the figure pointer folded in as the parenthetical (the
% fold licence); the sentence itself untouched per M-D.]
In a
flow, actions (the ATT\&CK techniques) are joined by \emph{effect}
edges that carry precondition semantics (a downstream action cannot
execute until the upstream one completes), with AND/OR operators
expressing multi-input dependencies and conditions capturing
environmental state \citep{ctid2025attackflow}
(Figure~\ref{fig:attack-flow-volt-typhoon}).
% [F3 cut: the figure-walk sentence. F4 cut: the terminal-condition
% block (the dictated slot 3 of 2026-09-03, its VERIFY watchlist and
% walk-grounding record with it --- full provenance in git history
% and handoff item 13). C2 cut: the flexibility recap. All three per
% the pass-5 log above.]
% [X1 MOVE pass 5: the corpus sentence up from below the (cut)
% terminal-condition block, so corpus -> curation reads causal.]
CTID maintains a corpus of such flows reconstructed by
analysts from publicly reported incidents
\citep{ctid2025attackflow}: reliable structured behaviour, bought at a
coverage cost.

Volt Typhoon had no example flow in this corpus, so one was curated
by hand from the joint advisory AA24-038A \citep{cisaaa24038a}.

The advisory states the ordering behind 57 of the flow's 73 edges; the

% optional) --- ctid2025attackflow carries it; attackflow.md
% extraction extended with the record (Concept 5). (b) Corpus half
% MEASURED against data/gap/_corpus_stix/ (38 flows): 36 carry no
% timestamps; two (MITRE NERVE, DFIR BumbleBee Round 2) populate
% execution_start with 1970-01-01 epoch placeholders --- clock times
% only, no real-world dates. Substance of "leaves them empty" holds
% for all 38 (no machine-usable real-world tempo anywhere); the
% literal wording is falsified at the margin. [3b] keep "leaves them

% empty", per the exit rework ruling. (c) Cite carrier:
% month-granularity campaign dates are a catalogue-page fact, so the
% cite moves mitre2026attackv19 -> mitre2026attackkb per your
% 2026-09-02 carrier ruling (references.bib comment). Do not
% re-flag.]
% [C4 pass 5: "Public incident corpora encode order, not tempo: "
% cut (duplicated the stitch sentence) and ", the exact intended home
% for this data" cut. Pass 6: "And what neither strand supplies is
% tempo" fused into this sentence's head at a colon (V8).]
The curated record supplies order and dependency, not tempo: the
Attack Flow schema defines optional per-action start and end
timestamps, but the public corpus leaves them all but empty, because
breach reports
rarely contain machine-usable timestamps; ATT\&CK campaign records
carry only month-granularity first- and last-seen dates
\citep{ctid2025attackflow, mitre2026attackkb}.
The apparatus for reconstructing behaviour from CTI, automated and
manual alike, sits within the threat-intelligence and
incident-response literature, not within MTD evaluation.

\section{MTD evaluation}
\label{sec:mtd-evaluation-survey}

% Strand opener (unnumbered, 1/4 unit). REWRITTEN 2026-09-07 (Marc's
% ruling on the cold read: the 2026-09-05 recast kept Cho's five-
% commitment anatomy plus a dispatch sentence and "in that order" ---
% the scaffolding cut from ch1 on 2026-08-21). Shape now mirrors the 3.1
% interstitial: S1 the strand's question, MARC'S DICTATION ("this
% section looks at how the field evaluates an MTD mechanism and the
% steps involved in such evaluation"; repaired: "dissection" -> "this
% section", "MTU" -> "MTD"; "looks at" -> "surveys" on his ruling the
% same day, matching 3.1's verb); S2 the sequence with the section
% references folded in as parentheticals (the ch2 MTDSim-preamble
% convention; the three nouns are the registry's ratified terms ---
% defence modelling approach / metrics / evaluation method --- so the
% thread and the headings agree). SESSION-ASSEMBLED S2 under the
% connective-prose green light, ratify-on-read. RULINGS, do not
% re-flag: the network model and attacker model are NOT named (ch2's
% preamble already frames MTDSim as network / defence / attacker;
% 3.3's interstitial opens on the attacker model); no citation (a
% signpost carries no cited claim --- the 3.1 rule; each subsection
% opens on Cho); no G1-style synthesis ("Each step carries assumptions
% of its own" was proposed as S3 and CUT by Marc as too strong);
% "evaluates ... evaluated ... evaluation" is a term repeat, ruled
% fine thesis-wide. Consequence noted: the dropped dispatch sentence
% was ch3's only statement of why the network model is not surveyed
% here --- the carrier is now ch2's preamble.
% SUPERSEDED (the 2026-09-05 recast, for reversibility): "An MTD
% evaluation commits to a network model, an attacker model, a set of
% defence mechanisms, a metric set, and an evaluation method
% \citep{cho2020}; the mechanisms are themselves designed under a
% defence modelling approach \citep[Sec.~VI]{cho2020}. Two of these are
% fixed by the simulator this thesis inherits, the network model
% (Section~\ref{subsec:network-model}) and the defence mechanisms
% (Section~\ref{subsec:defence-mechanisms}); the attacker model is
% Section~\ref{sec:mtd-attacker-models}'s. The three the field varies,
% the approach, the metrics, and the method, are surveyed here in that
% order." Before it, the ported sentence (Cho's own list, superseded
% 2026-09-05): "An MTD evaluation study selects a network setting
% (enterprise, cloud, software-defined, Internet of Things, or
% cyber-physical), adopts a threat model, deploys one or more MTD
% mechanisms, and assesses the result against a metric set using one of
% several validation methods \citep{cho2020}." Reorder ruled 2026-09-02
% (metrics before methods, Cho's Sec. VII -> VIII order) stands.
This section surveys how the field evaluates a defence mechanism and
the steps involved in such an evaluation. A defence mechanism is designed
under a defence modelling approach
(Section~\ref{subsec:defence-modelling-approaches}), instrumented with
metrics (Section~\ref{subsec:mtd-metrics}), and evaluated under an
evaluation method (Section~\ref{subsec:evaluation-methods}).

% 1/2 unit. NEW HEADING 2026-09-05 (Marc's ruling, item 3): Cho
% Sec. VI, the theoretical approaches the DEFENCE is designed under
% (game theory, genetic algorithms, machine learning), carried here as
% an evaluate-strand commitment, not an attacker-modelling one. Why it
% earns a heading when this thesis uses none of them: three later
% arguments depend on it (the metrics unit's "dependent on the defence
% modelling approach" sentence; the criterion's two Cho Sec. V-D
% asymmetries, learning and rational-actor, granted to the defender;
% the gap's "rationality without capability"). GUARDRAIL (Marc's trap,
% 2026-09-05): this unit names where the FIELD's attacker comes from;
% the sentence "this thesis uses none of these" never appears here ---
% positioning on the ladder is ch4's opening. Shape mirrors the
% metrics unit: P1 the what (moved verbatim from the deleted 3.3.1
% "Attacker modelling traditions"), P2 the so-what (the attacker each
% approach imports --- the re-homed G4b, Marc's dictation). Heading +
% registry term "defence modelling approach" ruled 2026-09-05: the
% qualifier says whose model it is; bare "modelling" is the attacker's
% word in this thesis.
\subsection{Defence modelling approaches}
\label{subsec:defence-modelling-approaches}

% DRAFT STATE (2026-09-06): DICTATED by Marc, repaired (pass 2+3a) and

% P1 --- the what. Question: what is a defence modelling approach, and
% which ones does the field use?
Cho et al.\ cluster defence modelling into game-theoretic,
genetic-algorithm and machine-learning approaches, with game theory
dominating by volume \citep{cho2020}.
% PASS 6 on §3.2 (2026-09-07, registry RATIFIED the same day, blanket
%   accept): \citet subject -> "Cho et al.\ ... \citep" --- the
%   census-majority form (13 sites); supersedes this unit's own pass-6
%   \citet choice of 2026-09-07.
Game-theoretic approaches involve an attacker and a defender as rational
players competing over the same resources.
% RE-DICTATED 2026-09-06 (pass-4 marker 1), repaired: "I'm operating over"
%   -> "over" (STT/disfluency); the "I don't know, something like that"
%   hedge dropped. SUPERSEDED: "... rational players, and the defence
%   switching strategy is chosen against an opponent's payoff." (inverted
%   Cho :475/:483). [3b] "over the same resources" --- Cho's contested
%   object is the attack surface / system configurations; your noun.
%   [3b minor] "amongst each other" for two players.
Genetic algorithms involve searching over defence configurations against
a fitness function of attack resilience, diversity in time and space.
% RE-DICTATED 2026-09-06 (pass-4 marker 2). SUPERSEDED: "...
%   configurations; the fitness function is attack resilience, diversity
%   in time and diversity in space." Indefinite "a fitness function" now
%   carries the Crouse-and-Fulp-is-one-instance point. [3b minor] the
%   doubled "over ... over" is as dictated.
Machine learning involves learning the defence strategy from the observed
state, or hindering the attacker's own learning.
Mechanisms that are not built using these approaches are subsumed by the
SDR operation alone \citep{cho2020}.
% RULED 2026-09-06 (pass-4 marker 3): "mechanisms" KEPT --- the
%   standardisation noun (defence mechanisms); Cho's "techniques" is the
%   how-to-move sense and he uses "MTD mechanism" interchangeably, so no
%   collision. Do not re-flag.

% P2 --- the import. Question: what attacker does each approach bring
% with it? The Tay sentence in the strand closer is this paragraph's
% worked example (one home; not repeated here).
In game theory the attacker is a utility maximiser: behaviour reduces to
the attacker's payoff function.
However, attackers are not necessarily rational or intelligent
\citep{cho2020}.
% RULED 2026-09-06 (pass-4 marker 4) --- SESSION-ASSEMBLED from Marc's
%   ruling keywords ("unrealistic incentives, unintelligent actors,
%   utility maximising, does not capture the complexity of an attack
%   campaign or long-term objectives"), RATIFY ON READ. The colon clause
%   was Cho's con (:508--510: low-intelligence mass; incentives not
%   compatible); the "and" clause left at pass 5 (block A). PASS 6
%   (2026-09-07, Marc's blanket accept): the colon clause CUT as a
%   restatement of its head --- the sentence is now the bare Cho :508
%   near-verbatim with the cite. SUPERSEDED: ": incentives may be
%   unrealistic and actors unintelligent".
In genetic algorithms there is no attacker as an agent: the attacker
lives in the fitness landscape.
In machine-learning approaches the attacker model is supplied by the
training environment, which is built on assumptions, at a high level of
abstraction, to allow reinforcement-learning agents to be trained
\citep{tay2024, ho2024}.
% [cite] tay2024 Sec. 7.1--7.2 (no real-world data; results "may not
%   generalise ... artifacts of small simulation space"); ho2024 Sec. 5.1
%   ("more complex adversaries --- only one type in the simulator"), 5.5
%   (no real-life scenario testing). Both carry the lineage's instance;
%   neither carries a claim about RL simulators in general, which is why
%   that generalisation is cut.
If attacker models have no agency and are based on unrealistic
assumptions, then the logic of the defence mechanisms is limited.

% SUPERSEDED 2026-09-04 (ported text, for reversibility):
%   Cho et al.\ partition the MTD metric space along two axes
%   (Table~\ref{tab:mtd-metrics}): \emph{perspective} (attacker-side or
%   defender-side) and \emph{purpose} (effectiveness, capturing how well
%   the MTD reduces attack success, and efficiency, capturing the cost
%   the MTD imposes) \citep{cho2020}. Hong et al.\ \citep{hong2018}
%   extend this partition with network-state-dynamic measures (marked
%   $\dagger$ in Table~\ref{tab:mtd-metrics}) that quantify how the
%   security posture shifts as MTD operations execute across consecutive
%   network states.
\subsection{Metrics}
\label{subsec:mtd-metrics}

% 3b WALK RULED 2026-09-04 (Marc, spoken; applied): generic second
% person -> we/our (option 1); "threat model or attacker model" ->
% "attacker model" (his thesis-wide standardisation ask; conflicts with
% the RATIFIED 2026-08-20 registry row, so applied HERE only and raised
% as a PROPOSED refinement in terminology.md; the other 17 tex
% occurrences are listed there for his ruling); "your respective" cut;
% "attacker or defence" -> attacker-side / defender-side (matches the
% table); "mode of evaluation" -> "mode of modelling" (his suggestion,
% ratify-on-read; note the traditions unit is 3.3.1, not 3.2.2);
% "game-theoretic" -> "game-theoretic approaches" (his noun); the
% First/Second/Third walk DROPPED, the three reasons kept as
% complementary clauses in one sentence (deletion + commas only);
% "largest persistent issue" cited to all three surveys; the
% cross-technique sentence replaced by HIS chat reword (ratify-on-read).
% Citation faithfulness checked against the source markdown, each
% noted at its sentence.
%
% P1 --- the what. Question: what instrument does an MTD evaluation read
% its verdict from, and how does the field organise it?
Metrics instrument defence mechanisms for whether they are effective
and efficient.
% PASS 6 V2 (2026-09-07, blanket accept): "involve instrumenting" ->
%   "instrument" --- register b5 and the fourth "X involve" skeleton
%   after 3.2.1's ruled three-beat walk.
% RULED 2026-09-04 (pass 4, items 2+3): "simulator" -> "model" (the
%   ladder is 3.2.2's; Marc: "you must first instrument your model with
%   metrics"); "our defence, network, or attacker model" -> "our defence
%   mechanisms" (Marc: "we are evaluating our defence mechanisms", not
%   the network or attack models); the effective-and-efficient
%   apposition assembled from his spoken words ("testing your defence
%   mechanism, are they efficient and are they effective") --- it also
%   seeds the purpose axis. Ratify-on-read. SUPERSEDED: "Metrics are a
%   precondition for MTD evaluation: typically we have to instrument
%   our simulator, run it and measure, and then from those values we
%   can judge our defence, network, or attacker model." The
%   metrics -> validation method -> modelling tradition -> attack model
%   line Marc described is carried by the strand opener (Cho's anatomy
%   sentence), not here.
Metrics can be split on perspective, attacker-side or defender-side,
and purpose, effectiveness or efficiency \citep{cho2020}, as used in
Table~\ref{tab:mtd-metrics}.
% RULED 2026-09-04 (pass 4, item 5): cite moved onto the axes clause
%   (Cho supplies the axes, not the enumeration --- the Hong / Ho /
%   Alavizadeh / HCR rows are not his); "metrics" -> "metric families"
%   to match the caption and the one-row-per-family design.
% cite check: both axes are Cho's Sec. VII structure (VII-A
%   effectiveness / VII-B efficiency, each split attacker's / defender's
%   metrics); "perspective" is Cho's own word (:574, :904); "purpose" is
%   the review's label, not Cho's. Attribution holds for the axes.
% CUT 2026-09-04 (pass 4, item 1, Marc): the Hong sentence in full ---
%   "Dynamic security metrics are metrics as a property of consecutive
%   snapshots of a network state \citep{hong2018}." (earlier form: "This
%   partitioning can be extended into dynamic security metrics, which
%   are ..."). Reason: with the extension frame gone (Hong 2018 precedes
%   Cho 2020; the frame was a flow device) the sentence names a family
%   and stops --- "cutting a bad sentence is cutting a bad sentence".
%   hong2018 stays cited on the P2 topic sentence, on two table rows,
%   and in G1 (:73). Hong's static/dynamic distinction (a property of
%   one configuration vs of the trajectory of configurations; not
%   runtime-vs-after-the-fact) is explained in the handoff item 20
%   trail if a leg is ever wanted. Reversible.
Attack success probability dominates the effectiveness metrics
\citep{cho2020}.
% PASS 6 V5 (2026-09-07, blanket accept): "In the field," DELETED ---
%   the scope residue pass 5 cut from 3.2.3 (P1); register b4.
% cite check: Cho :636 --- "a dominant metric used for the
%   EFFECTIVENESS of MTD", census of 27 papers 2011-2018.
% RULED 2026-09-04 (pass 4, item 4): scoped to the effectiveness
%   metrics (Cho's scope; efficiency scoped out); the 2011-2018 census
%   window left implicit. Closes the item-18 [3b].
This dominance is dependent on the defence modelling approach:
game-theoretic approaches report payoff and utility
\citep{cho2020}.
% PASS 6 V3 + V6 (2026-09-07, blanket accept): "This" -> "This
%   dominance" (naked referent, register h); "often look at" ->
%   "report" (h phrasal; "often" under-claimed Cho :636 "one of the
%   dominant metrics" and 3.2.1 dropped its "often" hedges).
% cite check: Cho :636 --- "due to a large volume of game theoretic
%   approaches ... the payoff or utility of attackers or defenders is
%   also one of the dominant metrics". Faithful; "approaches" is Cho's
%   noun too.
% RESOLVED 2026-09-05 (item 4): "mode of modelling" -> "defence
%   modelling approach", the registry term ruled the same day; 3.2.1
%   now precedes this sentence and introduces the term. Was: [3b]
%   "mode of modelling" --- your chat suggestion, ratify on read.

% P2 --- the so-what. Question: what does the state of the instrument
% mean for any evaluation built on it, and what distinction survives?
The primary issue facing defence mechanisms, in Jalowski et
al.'s reading \citep{jalowski2026}, is that they cannot be benchmarked,
a persistent finding \citep{hong2018, cho2020, jalowski2026}:
metrics are
produced on an ad hoc basis, there is little consensus on how to
measure and instrument, there are too many metric families with no
agreement between them, and there is no common benchmark to measure
effectiveness or efficiency.
% RULED 2026-09-04 (pass 4, M2): (b) the "largest" ranking ATTRIBUTED
%   to Jalowski ("primary issue in the current state of the art", :123;
%   "already been raised in existing surveys ... very little has been
%   done", :209 carries "persistent") --- the thesis's own ranking is
%   the attacker-model gap (3.3). Session wording, ratify-on-read;
%   SUPERSEDED: "The largest persistent issue facing MTD defence
%   mechanisms is that they cannot be benchmarked". (a) "there are few
%   available metrics to measure effectiveness" CUT as Hong 2018's
%   dated diagnosis (:75); replaced by Marc's spoken clause ("too many
%   families ... no agreement"), grounded jalowski :209 ("many authors
%   introduce their own metrics, which adds to the overall confusion")
%   and :35 ("no consensus regarding how to measure"). Ratify-on-read.
% cite check, clause by clause: "ad hoc" = jalowski2026 :123 ("ad hoc
%   methods provided by their own creators"); "little consensus" =
%   jalowski2026 :35 ("no consensus regarding how to measure");
%   "few available" = hong2018 :77 ("only a few security metrics
%   available to measure and compare"); "no standard metrics proposed"
%   = cho2020 :568. The three-survey cite on the topic sentence carries
%   "persistent" (2018 / 2020 / 2026). Faithful as a synthesis.
% RULED 2026-09-04: the near-verbatim Cho :568 clause reworded in
%   Marc's words ("no common benchmark that exists in the MTD
%   literature to measure effectiveness or efficiency"); cho2020 still
%   carries it via the topic-sentence cite.
% RULED 2026-09-04: topic sentence made impersonal in Marc's wording
%   ("facing MTD defence mechanisms is that they cannot be benchmarked").
% RULED 2026-09-04 (pass 4, M2c): "no common benchmark" is the ONE
%   carrier (Marc: "I like benchmark better"). CUT: "There is a lack of
%   a common framework \citep{jalowski2026}." (jalowski :118, still
%   cited via the topic sentence); the so-what's restating head "What
%   this means is that there is a lack of a benchmark, which makes"
%   trimmed to "This makes" --- deletion + joint, ratify-on-read.
This makes objective cross-evaluation of defence mechanisms intractable
\citep{jalowski2026}.
% PASS 6 V7 + registry (2026-09-07, blanket accept): "to date" DELETED

% RULED 2026-09-04: "intractable" stays. The closing verdict sentence
%   ("This is the main structural issue facing MTD literature today")
%   CUT --- Marc: the claim is already made and cited in the block's
%   first sentence; "today" not needed, the thesis is dated. Reversible.

The effectiveness metrics are dependent on how the attacker was
implemented, and therefore on the assumptions that underlie it
\citep{hong2018}.

% Layout (ruled 2026-09-04, variant D, unchanged): rotated purpose
% labels on the left, shaded with the row; Basis as the row key; the
% two perspectives as columns, a row = one measure read from each side
% (Cho's pairings ASP/DSP, MTTC/MTTF, the utilities, the costs; Cho's
% vastness-to-attack-surface link at his [114]; Zaffarano's mirrored
% Table 4); empty cell = no counterpart; zebra at black!5 continuous
% down the table; bold abbreviations; the rotated label sits in the
% LAST row of its block (negative multirow: -7 effectiveness,
% -3 efficiency). Needs xcolor[table] + multirow (preamble).
% Cell grammar (new): CITATIONS FOLLOW THE NAME THEY REPORT --- family
% name (ABBR) \citep{defining work, then the survey that lists it, then
% reporters}; variant name \citep{its reporters}. A key may sit by each
% name it reports (ho/tay by risk and by HCR); a group after a
% multi-name family means "reports at least one of these" (ho/tay
% report APE, not APV/APN). cho2020 is cited where Cho's list is the
% source of the family and no defining work is cited (ASP, DSP, the

  \begin{tabular}{@{}cP{1.9cm}P{6.1cm}P{6.1cm}@{}}
    \toprule
    & Measures & Attacker-side & Defender-side \\
    \midrule
    & success events
      & Attack success probability (\textbf{ASP}) \citep{evans2011, cho2020, kim2026, masud2025}; attack success \citep{zaffarano2015}; attack success rate (\textbf{ASR}) \citep{ho2024, tay2024}
      & Defence success probability (\textbf{DSP}) \citep{cho2020}; mission success \citep{zaffarano2015}; attack actions blocked \citep{brown2023}; detection rate \citep{he2025} \\
    & attacker time
      & Mean time to compromise (\textbf{MTTC}) \citep{mcqueen2006, zhang2023, ho2024, tay2024, sharma2025}; attack compromise duration (\textbf{ACD}) \citep{hong2018}; attack success time \citep{kim2026}
      & Mean time to failure (\textbf{MTTF}) \citep{cho2020}; reliability \citep{alavizadeh2022} \\
    & game payoff
      & Attack utility \citep{cho2020}
      & Defence utility \citep{cho2020} \\
    & attack paths
      & Attack path variation, number and exposure (\textbf{APV}, \textbf{APN}, \textbf{APE}) \citep{hong2018, ho2024, tay2024}; shortest attack path variability (\textbf{SAPV}) \citep{ho2024}
      & \\
    & configuration space
      & Attack surface \citep{manadhatawing2011tse, manadhatawing2011}; unpredictability \citep{cho2020}
      & Vastness \citep{cho2020} \\
    & system state
      & Attack confidentiality and integrity \citep{zaffarano2015}
      & System security: confidentiality, integrity, availability and degree of vulnerability \citep{cho2020}; risk \citep{alavizadeh2022, ho2024, tay2024, masud2025, sharma2025}; host or network compromise ratio (\textbf{HCR}, \textbf{NCR}) \citep{zhang2023, ho2024, tay2024} \\
    \rowgroup{7}{Effectiveness} & network-state change
      &
      & Periodicity \citep{cho2020}; MTD execution frequency (\textbf{MEF}) \citep{ho2024}; time since last MTD (\textbf{TSLM}) \citep{ho2024, tay2024}; host IP variability or variation (\textbf{IPV}) \citep{ho2024, masud2025} \\
    \midrule
    & resource spent
      & Attack cost or payoff penalty (\textbf{AC}) \citep{cho2020, alavizadeh2022, masud2025}; attack cost of exploitation (\textbf{ACE}) \citep{hong2018}; attempts required \citep{brown2023}; return on attack (\textbf{RoA}) \citep{cremonini2005, alavizadeh2022, ho2024, tay2024, masud2025}
      & Defence cost and system performance overhead \citep{cho2020, mendonca2023} \\
    & service to users
      &
      & Quality of service (\textbf{QoS}) \citep{cho2020, mendonca2023, kim2026} \\
    \rowgroup{3}{Efficiency} & network-state change
      &
      & Variant assignment cost and downtime (\textbf{NVC}, \textbf{NVDT}), edge variation cost and time (\textbf{EVC}, \textbf{EVT}) \citep{hong2018}; node replacement downtime \citep{tay2024} \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Evaluation methods}
\label{subsec:evaluation-methods}

%
% P1 --- the what. Question: what evaluation methods does the field
% have, and what orders them?
Cho et al.\ classify the evaluation methods into four categories:
analytical, simulation, emulation, and real testbed. The analytical
category splits into probabilistic and graphical security models
\citep{cho2020}.
% C6 (pass 4, APPROVED 2026-09-07): "graphical-security-model approaches"
%   -> "graphical security models" --- Cho's own noun (VIII-A-2, "GSM");
%   the reserved word "approach" deleted per the S4 ruling.
% S1/S2 --- the ported sentence split at "with" (participle -> finite
%   verb, the sentence's own noun as subject); comma list -> colon;
%   "validation methods" -> "evaluation methods" per the 2026-09-02
%   heading ruling (reversible; last prose occurrence). [term minor]
%   "graphical-security-model approaches" --- Cho's own noun (Sec.
%   VIII-A-2 title); registry reserves "approach" for the defence
%   modelling approach. Keep as cited phrasing, or "models"?
Moving from analytical models to a real testbed, flexibility and
validity rise. Scalability falls and cost climbs \citep{cho2020}.
% PASS 6 V4 (2026-09-07, blanket accept): "Each evaluation method has
%   its trade-offs." CUT --- a preview of the sentence that follows
%   (register e); trims the pass-4 C5 promise, which C5 itself keeps.
% C5 LANDED 2026-09-07 (Marc: "just write it in like that; the citation
%   is doing the heavy lifting"). Option 2 of three (table with Cho's
%   unrated cells shown / one ordering sentence / one sentence per
%   method); his first form ("emulation ... low flexibility") corrected
%   against Table VI (emulation is high validity AND high flexibility;
%   its con is scale). Session-assembled from Cho's cells, RATIFIED on
%   read. The single-sentence form ("Flexibility and validity rise from
%   analytical models through simulation and emulation to a real
%   testbed, and scalability falls and cost climbs with them") was
%   REJECTED by Marc as too much in too little; split into two, the
%   four-item sequence dropped (S1 carries the order). Grid backing:
%   flexibility and validity are fully ordered by Cho's comparatives;
%   scalability falls on three rated rungs (simulation unrated); cost
%   climbs on the endpoints (middle rungs unrated).
Simulation is high
flexibility, high level of abstraction, but low validity, whereas a
real testbed has high flexibility and validity but is not scalable and
is high cost \citep{cho2020}.

Simulation remains the dominant method \citep{cho2020}.

This dominance is because of the high flexibility, above all in
modelling attack behaviours \citep{cho2020}, and low barrier to entry.

However, as a trade-off, parameters that are not captured in a
simulator \citep{cho2020} are not accounted for in defence mechanism
logic.

\noindent
MTD evaluation is mostly one defence against a single or small set of
attacks \citep[Sec.~V-D]{cho2020}; evaluating multiple defence mechanisms
together is where the recent work sits \citep{alavizadeh2022, brown2023,
masud2025, tay2024, kim2026}.

\section{Attacker models in MTD}
\label{sec:mtd-attacker-models}

% Interstitial (unnumbered, ~1/2 unit). Review Sec. IV-A cut to its two

% attacker it claims to defend. Both surveys, however, stop at

% same as measuring how far short any given evaluation falls, and
% neither survey resolves the critique onto specific papers, nor onto
% a scale against which an individual threat model can be placed."

% interstitials: one sentence of purpose, one of parts by pointer. CUT
% the timeline ("six years apart", "in 2020", "again in 2026"): Table
% 3.3's years carry the durability of the critique and 3.3.1 P1 carries

% \citep{jalowski2026} document it again in 2026." and the stitch "This
% section draws the properties of a sophisticated attacker from the
% literature the surveys themselves draw on, and then measures."
This section surveys the attacker model that MTD evaluation is scored
over, which both MTD surveys name as a primary limitation of the field
\citep{cho2020, jalowski2026}.
Section~\ref{subsec:attacker-criterion} derives the properties of a
sophisticated attacker from the literature;
Section~\ref{subsec:recent-threat-models} scores recent evaluations
against them; Section~\ref{subsec:research-gap} states the gap.

% That is the rule the tex already follows (seven locator cites).
\subsection{Properties of a sophisticated attacker}
\label{subsec:attacker-criterion}

% P1 --- diagnosis. Q: what do the surveys say the attacker model lacks?
% Ported (review :153, :155), verified against the source markdown
% 2026-09-07 (Cho V-D :454-464; jalowski2026.md §4.3). Two cuts, both
% Marc's rulings: "; most MTD work instead pairs one mechanism against
% one attack path" (the defence half of the same V-D passage is the
% §3.2 strand closer's --- one home) and "six years later" (the
% interstitial's). JOINT session-chosen, ratify on read: "Jalowski et
% al.\ reach the same diagnosis through ..." (was "... six years later,
% through ..."). [term] "threat-modelling assumptions" paraphrases
% Jalowski's §4.3 title ("Threat Modeling: Deconstructing Attacker
% Assumptions") --- cited-title language under registry row 67; swap
% to "attacker-model assumptions" if the paraphrase does not earn the
% carve-out.
% OPENER --- SESSION-DRAFTED 2026-09-07 on Marc's green light ("if the
% opening is just prescriptive I'm happy for you to draft something
% up"), RATIFY ON READ. Claim-first (clears gate check 1's soft fail)
% and the unit's one pointer at the table, placed before the source
% walk so the reader knows the table is coming. The close keeps only
% the ownership flag so the two do not repeat (Marc: "it's kind of
% repetitive").
Table~\ref{tab:attacker-properties} lists the eight properties of a
sophisticated attacker, drawn from what the two surveys say MTD's
attacker models lack and from the APT definition of
Section~\ref{subsec:apt}.
Cho et al.\ name three under-developed dimensions of the attacker model
\citep[Sec.~V-D]{cho2020}. The first is the smart, learning-capable
attacker: where
defenders are routinely granted machine-learning capability, the
attacker is assumed to follow fixed patterns rather than to learn and
adapt. Cho et al.\ note that this asymmetry runs contrary to practice,
where the attacker is often the more sophisticated of the two. The
second is the scarcity of multi-strategy scenarios, in which attacker
and defender each branch across plural options. The third
is the asymmetric application of the rational-actor framing: the agent optimising for a cost-effective outcome is
modelled on the defender side and seldom on the attacker's. Jalowski
et al.\ reach the same diagnosis through a gap analysis of MTD, and
name the attacker model ``the most glaring flaw in the MTD literature''
\citep[p.~8]{jalowski2026}. Their critique is concrete about why: the
active-scanning baselines (e.g., Nmap) against which MTD is typically
tested are too naive for an APT attacker, which relies on passive
reconnaissance to stay hidden and learns the defender's
mutation patterns over time. The corrective they
prescribe is an attacker that reasons about the MTD scheme itself and
the logic driving its movement, not a scripted intruder
oblivious to the defence.

Cho et al.\ also name four characteristics of a sophisticated attacker
\citep[Sec.~V-A]{cho2020}, and hold that APT attackers, more or less,
show them: \emph{persistent}, operating across multiple stages, from
reconnaissance through exploitation, rather than as a one-time
intrusion; \emph{adaptive}, responding to changing system
and environmental conditions within its resource limits;
\emph{stealthy}, showing no identifiable attack behaviour until the
moment an attack is calculated to inflict serious harm;
and \emph{incentive-driven}, a rational actor sensitive to incentives
such as attack success at minimum cost. The NIST
definition of an APT attacker (Section~\ref{subsec:apt}) overlaps with
Cho et al.'s enumeration: persistence and adaptivity are integral to it \citep{nist2011sp80039}; Alshamrani et al.\ hold
that the objective it names is what conditions behaviour after the
foothold \citep[Sec.~II-C]{alshamrani2019}. The
selection of the eight and the merge of the definition onto the
enumeration are this thesis's own synthesis.

\begin{table}[tbp]
  \centering
  \caption[The eight properties of a sophisticated attacker]{The eight
  properties of a sophisticated attacker and their sources.}
  \label{tab:attacker-properties}
  \tablestyle
  \begin{tabular}{@{}rP{3.2cm}P{5.9cm}P{5.3cm}@{}}
    \toprule
    & Property & What it names & Source \\
    \midrule
    1 & Persistence & a campaign across multiple stages, pursued over an extended period & \citep[Sec.~V-A]{cho2020}; \citep[Sec.~II-A, II-C]{alshamrani2019}; \citep{nist2011sp80039} \\
    2 & Objective conditioning & behaviour after the foothold conditioned on the attacker's objective & \citep[Sec.~II-A, II-C]{alshamrani2019} \\
    3 & Strategic plurality & branching across plural attack options & \citep[Sec.~V-D]{cho2020} \\
    4 & Adaptivity & reaction to changing conditions and to the defender's resistance as they occur & \citep[Sec.~V-A]{cho2020}; \citep[Sec.~II-A]{alshamrani2019}; \citep{nist2011sp80039} \\
    5 & Stealth & evasion and passive reconnaissance & \citep[Sec.~V-A]{cho2020}; \citep[Sec.~II-A, II-C]{alshamrani2019}; \citep[Sec.~4.3]{jalowski2026} \\
    6 & Incentive-driven rationality & decisions conditioned on a cost/benefit signal & \citep[Sec.~V-A, V-D]{cho2020} \\
    7 & Learning & the defender's patterns learned over time and retained across deployments & \citep[Sec.~V-D]{cho2020}; \citep[Sec.~4.3]{jalowski2026} \\
    8 & Scheme awareness & a model of the MTD scheme itself and the logic of its movement & \citep[Sec.~V-D]{cho2020}; \citep[Sec.~4.3]{jalowski2026} \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Two works from 2025--2026 were selected using a keyword search for
MITRE ATT\&CK, as the works most likely to be grounded in attacker
modelling. The simulator lineage this thesis extends \citep{brown2023,
zhang2023, ho2024, tay2024} is scored in the same way.
Table~\ref{tab:attacker-cross-section} scores the three against the
eight properties of Section~\ref{subsec:attacker-criterion}.

MTDSim's attacker model is the baseline attacker of
Section~\ref{subsec:attacker-model}, carried unchanged through the
lineage (Table~\ref{tab:mtdsim-lineage}) except for Zhang et al.'s
exploitation-time rule \citep{zhang2023}. Brown et al.\ state that
``all attacker agents in the simulation will always follow the attack
procedure'' \citep[p.~7]{brown2023}. Six cells are partial. The
per-host loop continues across hosts and through disruptions (1); the
two objectives are fixed at design time (2); three attack vectors are
tried in a fixed fallback order (3); each kind of disruption triggers a
scripted re-scan, in Brown et al.'s word ``forced'' (4); exploits
within a host are ordered by return on attack (RoA), a cost/impact
ratio derived from Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) metrics
(6); and exploitation time is halved on a vulnerability exploited
before \citep{zhang2023} (7). Scanning is active and unconstrained,
with no detection model (5), and the attacker holds no model of which
mechanism runs or when (8).

% P3 --- Masud. A1 PORT masud2025.md l.186--188 (threat model, DB on
% host 8), l.414 (all_simple_paths, Algorithm 2), l.420/584--586 (per-
% path metrics before/after each deployment); A2 PORT l.299, verified
% p.7 (both quotes on the same page); B1 PORT the rule (caption) +
% l.188/414 (2); B2 EXISTS as the former DICTATE clause, now
% unconditional (handoff §10.10: strict NONE ruled): Eq.~4 l.420,
% Eq.~8 l.468 (the defender's selection). HARM defined in ch2.
In Masud et al., the attacker is a node of a three-layer HARM of an
IoT-cloud topology, joined to the entry virtual machines, against which
a hybrid MTD is evaluated \citep{masud2025}. At each interval the
evaluation enumerates every simple path from that node to the target,
the database on host 8, and recomputes a CVSS-derived success
probability, cost and RoA for each path before and after each MTD
deployment \citep[Algorithm~2]{masud2025}. The attackers are described
as ``modeled after techniques in the cyber kill chain and MITRE
ATT\&CK'' and their vulnerabilities as ``prioritized by return on
attack'' \citep[p.~7]{masud2025}. No attacker acts in the evaluation:
Algorithm~2 enumerates every path and scores it, and no step chooses
one, so no cell has anything to hold. The objective is the
enumeration's target node (2), and RoA is a per-path metric (Eq.~4)
consumed by the defender's selection (6).

% P4 --- Kim. A1 EXISTS ("genuinely" stripped; SDN expanded, first use
% in the thesis); A2 PORT kim2026.md l.382 (Poisson 0.2/min; Apache
% 2.4.49), l.386 (4096 addresses, five repeats), l.282 (four CKC stages,
% CVE-2021-41773), l.310/414 (shell held until the trigger severs it);
% A3 PORT l.416--418, verified p.9 (replaces "overlap ... out of scope",
% p.7, SUPERSEDED: "The work is itself candid that ``the overlap in time
% between each phase is out of scope'' \citep[p.~7]{kim2026}: the
% multi-phase attacker is, in execution, a sequential script across
% collapsed phases."); B1 PORT 1: l.276, 282, 414, 270 / 5: l.266 §5.2
% p.7, l.450 (no defence in the baseline) --- the former DICTATE clause
% absorbed; B2 PORT 3: l.276, 382 / 4: l.310, 388 / 2: l.382, 272 /
% 6: l.270, 386 / 7: l.270 / 8: l.270, 368.
Kim et al.\ deploy a multi-phase MTD framework on a physical
software-defined networking (SDN) testbed, mapping MTD sub-techniques
across the Cyber Kill Chain and onto ATT\&CK techniques \citep{kim2026}.
The attacker is an automated script launched as Poisson arrivals that
scans 4096 virtual addresses for port 80 with up to five repeats,
fingerprints the web service, fires the public exploit for
CVE-2021-41773 where Apache 2.4.49 is found, and holds a reverse shell
until the next MTD trigger severs it: four kill-chain stages in fixed
order, labelled with ATT\&CK technique identifiers
\citep[Secs.~5.2, 6.1]{kim2026}. Kim et al.\ state the assumption:
``we assume that the attackers follow the predefined sequence of
phases based on CKC'' \citep[p.~9]{kim2026}. Two cells are partial.
The four stages run in sequence in one process with a
measured retention time, each arrival starting without network
knowledge (1), and the outbound shell is chosen ``in order to evade the
detection by a defense system'' \citep[p.~7]{kim2026} on a testbed that
runs no detector (5). One scenario, one exploit and one target service
(3); the script does not observe the trigger that severs it (4); one
objective and one fixed action after the foothold (2); the repeat cap
is the experimenters' budget (6); nothing is carried across arrivals
(7); and the scan range is sized to the MTD interval by the
experimenters (8).
